(The hearing commenced at 10:00 a.m.) 
A.L.J. KERSEY:  Good morning.  I call 
case numbers 25-E-0072 and 25-G-0073, proceedings on 
the motion of the Commission as to the rates, 
charges, rules, and regulations of Consolidated 
Edison Company of New York, Inc. for gas and electric 
service.   
My name is Tara Kersey.  I am an 
administrative law judge with the Department of 
Public Service.  With me today are Administrative Law 
Judges James Costello and Nicholas Planty.  Together, 
we are presiding over these proceedings.  We are here 
this morning for an evidentiary hearing that was 
noticed by the secretary on November 10th, 2025.  At 
today's hearing, we will receive into evidence the 
Joint Proposal that was filed in these proceedings on 
November 5th, 2025, as well as other exhibits and to 
admit certain testimony and allow cross-examination 
of certain witnesses.   
We will start by taking appearances.  
And to streamline the process, we will call the 
parties and ask that the party representative 
identify themselves for the record.   
Ildiko, our court reporter, to the

Q. 
What is a management and operations audit?  
A. 
Management and operations audits review a utility’s 
construction program planning process, operational 
efficiency, and other matters.    The Commission 
conducts comprehensive management and operations 
audits of large, investor-owned utilities, including 
Con Edison, on a five-year cycle.  The Commission 
also conducts focused operations audits of specific 
topics as needed.

rate proceedings for the Company in Cases 22-E-0064 
and 22-G-0065.

Please describe Recommendation VII-2.

Recommendation VII-2 is that Con Edison perform an 
assessment of estimating accuracy for large capital 
projects to identify potential improvements.  In the 
Company’s December 31, 2024, Implementation Plan 
update, Con Edison reported that it completed 
implementing this recommendation on November 30, 
2024.

Why is Recommendation VII-2 relevant to these rate 
proceedings?

the HTY, any resulting changes to estimating cost 
assumptions, associated improvements, and resulting 
benefits or savings would not be reflected in the

Q. 
Please describe Recommendation IX-3.  
A. 
As noted on page A-64 of NorthStar’s Final Report, 
Recommendation IX-3 is that Con Edison develop a 
process for identifying, measuring, and tracking 
actual benefits and savings achieved from information 
technology projects.  In the Company’s March 31, 
2025, Implementation Plan update, Con Edison reported 
that it continues to consider whether to adopt the 
resulting process more widely.

Q. 
Why is Recommendation IX-3 relevant to these 
proceedings?  
A. 
During the audit, NorthStar found that Con Edison was 
implementing a methodological approach to more 
accurately tracking the value realized from system 
implementation.  NorthStar states that a formal 
process such as the one being implemented by Con 
Edison would enable more accurate estimates, 
tracking, and reporting of savings and benefits from 
capital projects.  Because Recommendation IX-3 will 
be implemented during the bridge period between the 
HTY and the RY, any resulting changes, associated 
improvements, and resulting benefits or savings would 
not be reflected in the HTY.

which is discussed in the Staff Accounting Panel 
testimony.  Given the timing of implementation, we 
would expect the efficiency gains under 
Recommendations VII-2 and IX-3 to materialize during 
the RY.  However, these improvements are difficult to 
identify and quantify with precision, and are,

A.

the Commission might conduct during the rate plan for 
recovery in Con Edison’s next base rate cases.  
Q. 
Do you agree with the Company’s proposed treatment of 
management and operations audit costs?  
A. 
Yes, but with some limits.  We agree that the 
Commission should allow Con Edison to defer the cost 
of comprehensive management and operations audits 
should the Company incur such costs during its rate 
plans.  However, we recommend that the recovery of 
any deferral be limited to the costs allowed pursuant 
to the contract.

A.L.J. KERSEY:  I just want to give a 
reminder on the standard.  The issue for a proposed 
settlement is whether that settlement is in the 
public interest.  While opposing parties are able to 
seek information from the sponsoring parties on 
individual provisions of a settlement, the Commission 
considers the agreement as a whole, aware that the 
proposal is the product of negotiation among 
different interests and concerns. 
The burden of supporting a proposed 
settlement is on the settling parties, and so those 
parties are able to define the witnesses that they 
choose to use sponsor a Joint Proposal. 
In addition, sponsoring parties may 
put forward witnesses as one panel, or they may 
choose to have their witnesses appear as an 
individual party. 
As a final reminder, cross-examination 
on legal conclusions, interpretation, and theory is 
improper.  Such clearly legal material is for 
briefing and argument only.  Even where a witness 
makes a claim that a response is based on the advice 
of counsel, the legal opinion of witnesses have no 
evidentiary value for a number of reasons, including

Q.  
Have you previously testified before the New York 
State Public Service Commission?  
I have not testified before the Public Service 
Commission in a rate case, but I have presented 
cases in the Informal Hearing setting on behalf of 
clients of UtiliSave.  
Q.   What is the purpose of your testimony?  
To advocate for the PSC to mandate that Con Ed be 
required to provide all the data and formulas 
related to each charge and component of a customer’s 
bill, such that there is full transparency as to the 
method of formulation and calculation with 
provisioning of all bill inputs which are utilized 
to derive every billing component.     
Q. 
What are the major findings of your testimony in 
these proceedings?

My testimony examines and provides evidence that the 
Joint Proposal fails to take into account that Con 
Edison’s billing strategy and policies are not as 
transparent as they should be in order to protect 
ratepayers’ interests. The Joint Proposal only 
provides for a limited expansion in billing 
transparency, that being related to taxation (see 
Joint Proposal at 93). Based on my 35years of 
experience in the industry, and my interactions with 
multiple utilities, much more in the way of 
informational transparency is required to fulfill 
the needs of the public in this critical area. My 
testimony will also highlight examples where the 
lack of data transparency inhibits the ability of a 
customer to determine the accuracy of the bills 
provided to them by Con Edison.  
Q. 
Is greater information transparency and greater 
transparency in the billing process in the public 
interest?  
  
Yes.  
Q.  
Why is it important that the information you are 
requesting be made available?  
  
There are a number of reasons:  
A. 
There should be a sustained practice of

B. 
Given all the variables that go into a correct 
bill, it is very possible for there to be a mistake 
on how an input is allocated. Once example is in the 
calculation of the BTU heating factor, there are 5 
zones that serve all customers, the billing does not 
disclose which zone the customer has been placed on, 
how can the customer check if he is in the correct

C.

each component of their bill and all the related 
inputs should be provided so that this is possible. 
If there is a charge the support/backup for this

required to pay for charges that are not verifiable? 
There is really no aspect of commerce where it would

the accuracy of any component of the bill, would not

but just illustrative. Gas Cost Factor: The 
Company’s Gas Cost Factor is made up of the average 
variable cost and the average fixed cost of gas both 
of which have multiple sub-components. These 
components are set out in Con Edison’s gas tariff at 
Leaves 155.1, 156, 157, 157.1, 158, 159, 160, 161. 
The values, data and formulas used to derive these 
sub-components are not disclosed to ratepayers. When 
UtiliSave filed an Information Request seeking 
information on Con Edison’s policy regarding 
disclosure of these subcomponents, Con Edison 
replied that it did not propose to change its 
policy. Con Edison replied that such information was 
confidential, and without proof, alleged that 
disclosure of such data ” could impair the Company’s 
position in any future negotiations with gas 
suppliers and service providers with respect to 
hedge-based gas transactions.”

based on Con Edison’s forecast of NYISO market 
energy prices plus a defined ‘Hedging Impact.' The 
cost per kWh is reconciled monthly via the 
Adjustment Factors – MSC, with true-ups reflected in

the critical need for utility transparency.   
S8530, entitled “The Utility Transparency And Fair 
Billing Protection Act” seeks to prevent consumers 
from being overcharged due to misclassified utility 
rates by mandating proactive account reviews, 
automatic adjustments, and ensuring clear 
communication between utilities and customers.  
S8128 entitled the “Ratepayer Transparency Act” 
which requires bills utilized by public and private 
gas corporations, electric corporations and gas and 
electric corporations in levying charges for service

S9188 entitled “AN ACT to amend the public service  
law,  in  relation  to  information required  to be 
published prior to a major rate change by a public 
gas or electric utility” became effective on 
November 24, 2025. This Act requires the public 
service commission to publish certain information 
prior to a major rate change by a public gas or 
electric utility including an explanation of why the 
rate change is requested and a summary of how the 
proposed revenue will be spent. The bill, now law, 
specifically requires the Public Service Commission 
to “within ten days of the  commencement of  any  
matter  involving  a  major  change  in  a rate for 
electric or gas service, publish, in a readily 
accessible location on  the  department's website,  
a  summary  in  plain language, not to exceed one 
page or five hundred words…” containing information 
related to the rate change proceeding so that 
ratepayers can evaluate the potential effect on 
their utility costs, among others.
